GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 74
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of
FP181
Proposal name: CRAFT - Catalytic Capital for First Private Investment Fund for
Adaptation Technologies in Developing Countries
Accredited entity: Pegasus Capital Advisors
Project/programme size: Large
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
Scale: Medium
1.1 ImOpvaecrtv ipeowt ential
1.1.1
1.
Technology innovation is a critical element in addressing climate challenges, both in
mitigation and adaptation, as recognized by the Conference of the Parties to the United Nations
Framework Convention on Climate Change. Systematically addressing these challenges needs to
involve the private sector, as the challenges posed by the climate crisis are now at a scale that
goes beyond what development finance institutions and governments can achieve on their own.
Nevertheless, in 2019 only 6 per cent of venture capital funding went into climate technology.
While there is a substantial promise that more will be done, there is no certainty that this will
happen. As such, the creation of a dedicated fund structure in the form of CRAFT is welcomed, as
it will help address crucial technology gaps including those faced by developing countries in
th eir adaptation to the climate crisis.
2.
The CRAFT Fund proposes to address adaptation challenges in 11 developing countries
(Bahamas, Brazil, Colombia, India, Kenya, Malaysia, Mexico, Morocco, Rwanda, South Africa and
Trinidad and Tobago), of which only six (Bahamas, Brazil, Mexico, Rwanda, South Africa,
Trinidad and Tobago) had provided no-objection letters (NOL) at the time of finalising this
assessment. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) notes that the rationale for
country selection is not clear in some cases, as some of the countries targeted are neither first
nor second priorities of the Climate Policy Initiative study that was provided to serve as
demonstration of feasibility, thus raising questions as to whether they have the absorption
ca pacity for the kind of technologies CRAFT is targeting.
3.
CRAFT is expected to directly deliver close to USD 400 milion in financing for adaptation
purposes through investments in technology companies with between USD 5 million and USD
100 million annual revenue and undertake activities in six technology areas with potential
cli mate adaptation use, including:
(a)
agricultural analytics;
(b)
water harvesting and drip irrigation;
(c)
resilient food systems;
(d)
geospatial mapping and imaging;
(e)
supply chain analytics; and
(f)
catastrophe risk modeling.

=== PAGE 78 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 75
4.
The TAP notes that some of these technology categories have excessive ranges of
coverage and could include high greenhouse gas-emitting technologies (e.g. cold chains based
on fossil fuels) and/or support maladaptive approaches (utilizing geospatial technology for
more aggressive water resource exploitation). It is not clear what safeguards would be in place
to prev ent Ethliegsieb irliistkys c frriotmer biae ing realized.
1.1.2
5.
When acting through a structure in which GCF is several steps removed from action on
the ground, with a wide remit both sectorally and geographically, the eligibility criteria for the
CRAFT Fund are the crucial elements that would allow the TAP and eventually the Board to
assess whether the investment will be aligned with the requirements of GCF, for example, as
expressed in the Governing Instrument. The TAP notes in this regard that the initial submission
of the documentation was missing critical information on eligibility criteria, and no final agreed
set of these criteria had been received at the time of drafting this assessment. Noting the draft
criteria, which were later provided in the form of a draft term sheet contained in annex 14, a
nu mber of challenges are observed.
companies to be selected
6.
CRAFT will utilize two sets of eligibility criteria. First, have to
co nform to all three criteria set out below in terms of their characteristics:
(a)
Increases climate resilience: Company offers a technology, product or service that helps
its customers address a climate vulnerability, risk or impact and build resilience (i.e. a
“climate resilience solution” aligned with the Adaptation Solutions Taxonomy);
(b)
Growth stage: Company is at the growth stage, typically generating approximately USD 5
million to 100 million in annual revenues with demonstrated customer adoption; and
(c)
Environmental, social and governance (ESG) and impact metrics: Company is committed
and able to manage ESG risks and to track and report on impacts consistent with the
environmental and social management system and Impact Measurement System (IMS)
of CRAFT.
7.
These criteria define the type of company CRAFT will consider investing in. The TAP
notes in this regard that, by setting the upper limit at USD 100 million in annual revenues,
CRAFT includes such a considerable target range that the limit is almost meaningless. For
example, geospatial mapping company Waze had revenues of USD 37.7 million when it was
acquired by Google for USD 1.2 billion. A lower target range would give the TAP more comfort in
te rms of the additionality of the GCF investment, particularly in light of the discussion below.
companiesto be selected
8.
A second set of eligibility criteria further aims to ensure country ownership by detailing
ch aracteristics and adding an activity-related element for the .
9.
The TAP understands these are draft criteria subject to further negotiation at this stage,
and assumes that “NOL country” refers to countries eligible to receive GCF funds that have
iss ued an NOL, as set out in paragraph 2 of this assessment.
10.
Only one of the four criteria needs to be met in order for a company to be eligible for
CR AFT investment, and they are provided below:
(a)
The company is headquartered in an NOL country (including subsidiaries and joint
ventures); or
(b)
The majority (more than 50 per cent) of the company’s revenue or cost is generated or
incurred in NOL countries; or
(c)
The majority (more than 50 per cent) of the company’s employees are located in NOL
countries; or

=== PAGE 79 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 76
(d)
The company commits to dedicate a substantial portion of invested GCF proceeds to
increase employment, development impact or measurable climate adaptation or
mitigation benefits in NOL countries.
11.
In a discussion on these with the accredited entity (AE) and the executing entity (EE),
the latter noted that there is also an overall investment framework that would assure the
ach ievement of adaptation outcomes.
12.
In relation to the first set of criteria, the value of the Adaptation Solutions Taxonomy is
acknowledged as the first filter to confirm that a particular technology constitutes a “resilience
technology solution”. Unlike mitigation technologies, however, adaptation technologies are
country and location-specific and the successful transfer of such technologies to a developing
country context faces a lot of barriers. It is therefore necessary to consider the context in which
any new technology will be purchased and utilized. For example, a water-efficient irrigation
system is not in and of itself a mechanism for adaptation. In some contexts, it might be
purchased purely to reduce input costs. It becomes an adaptation mechanism only if utilized in
the context of water scarcity, and only where this scarcity arises, at least in part, because of
climate change (as opposed to, for example, population growth). By the same logic, geosynthetic
products, while useful in flood control mechanisms, have other purposes. Even when used in
flood control, the products may be managing a level of flooding that had not been observed to
increase in recent decades and is not projected to increase in coming decades as a result of
cli mate change.
13.
In relation to point (d) in the second set of criteria above, the TAP notes that the word
“substantial” is too vague for use in a measurable eligibility criterion, especially when combined
with the broad scope of “increase employment, development impact or measurable climate
ad aptation or mitigation benefits”.
14.
Following interaction with the AE and the EE, we note that points (a), (b) and (c) relate
to the investment screening process and describe conditions that must be in place at the time of
selection of the company for investment. Point (d), on the other hand, would relate to future
actions, and is thus not relevant for screening. For both these reasons, the TAP strongly suggests
that point (d) be removed. Point (d) is also rendered irrelevant by covenant 17 (j) in the draft
ter m sheet (annex 14 to the funding proposal)—see paragraph 17 below.
15.
We also indicate our concern that, based on the current two sets of criteria, it cannot be
assured that GCF proceeds will be utilized to create climate adaptation benefits. In particular,
the TAP notes that these criteria do not seem to consider the possibility that companies have
more than one product under development or on offer, with one or more of them not climate-
related and/or that the product on offer has multiple uses, not all of which are related to climate
adaptation—a particular risk in geoinformation, supply chain analysis and catastrophe risk
mo delling.
16.
For example, it is possible that a company is compliant with the first set of criteria and,
in terms of the second set, is based in South Africa and has most of their
revenue/cost/employees there, but is not engaging in meaningful climate-related activities as
their product has dual use or they have more than one product. Another possible example is a
company based in the United States of America that is compliant with the first set of criteria and
commits to invest an undefined “substantial” amount of GCF proceeds in an NOL country—for
example, to build schools or invest in e-scooters—which would make it compliant with
pa ragraph 10 (d) while not delivering adaptation benefits.
17.
The TAP noted earlier that point (d) in the second set of criteria may be effectively
rendered null and void by covenant 17(j) in the draft term sheet, which states that “The
Accredited Entity covenants that, as from the effective date, it shall: Contractually ensure that
the CRAFT Fund uses GCF Proceeds only makes investments in the Host Countries”. Although
there is a grammatical error in the clause, we assume the intention is to convey that the GCF

=== PAGE 80 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 77
proceeds in the CRAFT Fund will only be used for investments in the host countries or, to put it
differently, that 100 per cent of GCF proceeds will be used to make investments in the host
co untries, i.e. GCF-eligible countries that have issued NOLs.
18.
If this is the intention, we would welcome this, since such an approach would maximize
the sustainable development co-benefits of the adaptation investment. For the purposes of this
assessment, however, we have assumed that the two sets of crietria, including paragraph 10 (d),
rem ain in place.
19.
Once the investments have been made, there are also more active ways to steer the
actions of companies, e.g. through strong covenants on the use of GCF funds invested and/or
through supervision executed by a company’s Board. In this regard, the TAP notes that CRAFT
only “seeks” to obtain a seat on the Board of investee companies, as noted by the AE in their
response to our questions. It is however our view that, in order to ensure that investments
supported by GCF are compliant with GCF objectives, CRAFT obtaining a seat on the Board of
investee companies should be a non-negotiable condition for CRAFT’s investment in a
compa ny—Ctlhimroautgeh i mwhpiaccht i t would gain access to GCF resources.
1.1.3
20.
In order to make an assessment of the programme’s likely impact potential for climate
change adaptation, the TAP has reviewed the proposed processes whereby investments will be
selected, justified, set up to be monitored and monitored, and has found these to be positive but
no t yet rigorous enough to enable a solid climate rationale for the programme as a whole.
21.
In response to questions from the TAP, the AE has clarified that the process that CRAFT
uses to establish its “climate adaptation eligibility” is integrated into CRAFT’s formal investment
process. Following the testing of alignment of a candidate company’s technology/services/
products with the Adaptation Solutions Taxonomy mentioned above, the process involves an
Investment Committee screening note that evaluates the specific potential issues for an
investment. A final Investment Committee memo then summarizes the due diligence completed,
including a description of projected impact, with sections on “Climate Impact (hazard, exposure,
or vulnerability addressed” and “Intervention/Action (adaptation, mitigation [sic] benefits)”.
Mention is also made of a “Theory of Change describing the potential climate adaptation
impacts”. The TAP notes that the screening considering mitigation benefits is aligned with the
eligibility criterion in paragraph 10.(d) above, and thus does not provide in and of itself an
ad ditional safeguard to ensure a focus on adaptation benefits only.
22.
Following the selection of a company for investment, the process of establishing key
performance indicators (KPIs) includes seven indicator types, of which three to five need to be
chosen. Following a question from the TAP, the EE, Lightsmith, has reassured us that one of
these indicators will be on “Measuring Climate Change Adaptation and Climate Resilience
Impacts” and will not be optional. Annex 25 to the Impact Measurement System notes that
“Lightsmith utilizes a logic model to estimate the direct climate change adaptation impacts
derived from the availability and use of the intelligence, product or service. This model helps
identify the data to be collected and the KPIs to be monitored as part of the impact
measurement process, such as the number of units in use, the number of customers using it, or
the extent or value of assets benefiting from the use (e.g. hectares of land under drip irrigation).
Climate change adaptation measures should link to the specific hazard or exposure that is
incorporated in the vulnerability assessment.“ It is unclear to which vulnerability assessment
this refers. Lightsmith then monitors both environmental and social risks and KPIs throughout
the holding period of the investment, and the results of tracking the indicators are included in a
simple table containing “impact”, “indicator” and “value” in the annual ESG and impact reporting
template.

=== PAGE 81 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 78
23.
This further detail is welcomed by the TAP as it reflects the firm intention to select
companies for investment that can help fulfil the GCF’s mandate to provide support to
developing countries to adapt to the impacts of climate change, and to measure and track that
impact effectively. We understand that, for CRAFT and Lightsmith, these processes would be
sufficient as part of the CRAFT Fund. Nevertheless, to ensure high impact potential that would
justify the GCF investment, the AE will appreciate that the process in relation to adaptation
impact will need to go further and be more comprehensive than the process for other metrics.
We note that the AE has stated in the responses that the EE “welcomes technical guidance from
the GCF as it seeks to continually refine its approach”.
24.
The TAP understands that the precise market geographies within the NOL-issuing
countries (and beyond) cannot be known ahead of time, thus precluding the development of a
detailed adaptation rationale with full scientific underpinning, detailing observed historical
trends aligned with future projections in relation to key climate parameters, as is usually
expected in justifying any GCF adaptation investment. Although the Adaptation Solutions
Taxonomy clearly identifies technologies with potential to help vulnerable communties and
societies to adapt to negative climate change impacts, it is the context that turns a particular
technology (which could be business as usual technology) into an adaptation mechanism—for
example, not the drip irrigation system per se but the climate change-intensified drought
co ntext into which it is introduced.
25.
In order to provide as much rigour as possible, it would be helpful to explore how to
enhance the existing investment screening and KPI-planning processes already formulated by
the AE and the EE and/or develop an additional ex-ante assessment tool for the climate
adapation impact of an investment by CRAFT that would enable the weaving together of the
elements of climate impact, the technology’s interventions and the tracking of the results. This
could be achieved, for example, through a systematic conceptualization process like this:
Information on company and technology

26.
In the absence of such a systematic approach to selection and impact monitoring for
adaptation specifically, it is not possible for the TAP to be confident in the project’s adaptation
impact potential to the degree routinely expected by the GCF Board. Once such an approach has
been put in place, Pegasus should report this to GCF during the "due diligence" phase of any

1.1.4
28.
Overall, in line with the discussion above and other concerns set out below, but
considering the application of the proposed conditions and covenants, the TAP considers that
the impact potential of CRAFT is MEDIUM.
Scale: Medium
1. 2 Paradigm shift potential
29.
The paradigm shift of the programme is expected to be derived from the creation of a
fund dedicated to investing in adaptation technology providers. The TAP notes that while this is
an element of paradigm shift in adaptation, it is a necessary but insufficient condition, and the
loc alization of such technologies needs to be a critical element.
30.
Given the areas of investment set out above, as well as strengthened overall governance
and robust eligibility assessments, it is possible that, if investments focus on the climate element
of these technology areas and the countries that have issued NOLs, paradigm shift could be
ach ieved.
31.
Nevertheless, given the concerns about the governance and eligibility criteria, the TAP
does not have confidence that the chosen approach will deliver paradigm shift in GCF eligible
countries in general and in the NOL-issuing countries in particular, unless the proposed
conditi onsR ainsdk sco tvoe pnaarnatsd iagrme a sphpilfite d.
1.2.1
32.
The TAP observes the following challenges with this approach that, together, lead to a
we ak consideration of localization and specific response to adaptation needs:
(a)
Weak eligibility criteria aimed more at maximizing investment opportunities rather
than pursuing climate impact in NOL countries;
(b)
Weak governance arrangements that do not guarantee any influence in ensuring that the
direction of investments supported by GCF remains compliant with GCF objectives; and
(c)
Lack of a robust ex-ante assessment of the adaptation impact potential prior to

In view of the above discussion on impact potential and balancing the overall impact
potential and the risks, the TAP rates CRAFT’s paradigm shift potential as MEDIUM based on the
available information and if the the proposed conditions and covenants are applied.

1. 3 Sustainable development potential
Taken together, the prior
34.

.” The TAP cannot agree with this view,
given the observed weakness of the description of how GCF investment criteria will be met and
the questions around achieving climate impact.
35.
For example, the document labelled feasibility study is a Global Environment Facility
project report. Section V of that document, which should contain the key "experiences and
lessons learned", is empty. Annex 2b is a PowerPoint presentation that does not appear to be
designed to address feasibility questions for the GCF investment. For instance, slide 10 on
inv estment criteria did not cover country ownership considerations.
36.
An additional report by the Climate Policy Initiative's (CPI) Climate Innovation Lab that
dates back to 2017 has been provided. This report does not mention any of the countries that
have provided signed NOLs at the time of submission of the funding proposal to the TAP as first
or second priority countries. It also does not consider the volume proposed here (USD 400
million), instead proposing a total volume of USD 150 million of commercial investment for the
developing country sleeve—in this context, focusing on markets such as China as a priority,
wh ich has far higher absorption capacity than the countries that have issued NOLs combined.
37.
This absence of a dedicated market study to provide a baseline for the investments to be
supported creates substantial uncertainty about the market demand for the services to be
provided by the companies to be invested in; issues such as service affordability, technical
readiness for service use, maintenance and repair issues and so forth are left open. Four of the
six technology categories represent relatively high-tech and costly solutions that require a
supportive enabling environment, such as excellent rural connectivity for agricultural analytics,
inf luencing the scope of potential demand.
38.
At this stage of an assessment by the TAP, we would normally discuss the co-benefits of
the proposed GCF investments. In the absence of a dedicated baseline study and a detailed
market assessment of investment potential in the NOL countries, this would be a meaningless
exercise. The TAP notes the general information provided by the AE in section D.3 of the funding
proposal, and agrees that it is possible that these co-benefits could be achieved, depending on
which of these approaches is applied and where. It is however also possible that the CRAFT
Fund w oulSdu ssutpapinoartb glere Deenvheoluospem gaesn-te mPoistseinotni ainl tReantsiinveg and maladaptative investments.
1.3.1
39.
In view of the above discussion on impact potential and balancing the overall impact
potential and the risks, while considering the addition of the proposed conditions and
covenants, the TAP rates CRAFT’s sustainable development potential as MEDIUM.

GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 81
Scale: Uncertain
1. 4 Needs of the recipient
40.
Considering that the market for the six adaptation technology areas is worldwide and
that there is a growing urgency to adapt to the climate crisis, there is no doubt that the needs of
developing country recipients are high, as set out in technology needs assessments and
technology action plans, which foresee billions of USD of investment requirement in adaptation
tec hnology solutions.
41.
In this regard, a private-sector driven programme explicitly focusing on building the
domestic response in developing countries to develop affordable and appropriate technology
solutions for climate change adaptation that are designed to respond to deteriorating local
conditions, barriers and needs would be a highly welcome addition to the GCF portfolio. This
could involve providing finance to acquire licences to manufacture locally (real technology
transfer) and adapting processes to supply as many parts as possible locally and import as
needed. Such a programme would have many advantages, not just in terms of value addition, job
creation, development of indigenous small and medium-sized enterprises, preserving foreign
exchange reserves and ensuring affordability, but could also drive market transformation and
pa radigm shift for climate change adaptation in the developing countries themselves.
42.
Nevertheless, the TAP would refer to the discussions under sections 1.1. and 1.5 of this
assessment, which relate to the inability to specifically assess the needs of recipients in the
countries that have issued NOLs based on the currently proposed governance and eligibility
criteria, and the brief, high-level information provided by the AE in annex 24 to the funding
proposal to establish the climate challenges in the NOL countries. The information presented in
the table of “Eligibility criteria for programmatic adaptation projects” in section B.1 of the
funding proposal is purely indicative, and provides neither eligibility criteria nor an assessment
of actual climate vulnerabilities of specific groups of target customers and end users.
43.
Thus, even assuming that the range of safeguards not mandated in the funded activity
agreement ensure CRAFT investment in adaptation technologies only, the TAP notes that
according to the draft termpl sahneet the companies that will be invested in can legitimately be
headquartered in developed countries and sell into many markets, both GCF eligible and
ineligible, as long as they to sell into NOL-issuing countries as well. However, evidence that
markets exist in these countries for the more expensive products in particular has not been
sh own.
44.
Another reason for the uncertainty of the adaptation benefits serving to address the
need of the recipients is the fact that the second set of criteria apply only at the time CRAFT
makes the decision to invest. A company may, at the time of receiving the CRAFT investment, be
generating over 50 per cent of its revenue from sales to NOL-issuing countries, but may use the
investment to expand its sales operations into other markets in non-NOL countries and even
non-developing countries. This is, in the view of the TAP, a high risk as the technologies targeted
include a wide range of products—from very expensive high-tech technology like precision
agriculture equipment and remote sensing services, which may not have a big market in the
NOL countries, to lower-tech technology like rainwater harvesting, which can be (and are)
produc ed mNoereed esf ofifc itehnet lrye acnipdi eanffto rrdaatibnlyg locally.
1.4.2
45.
As a detailed assessment at country/sector level is not possible at this stage, the TAP is
currently not in a position to rate the specific needs of the recipients in relation to the proposed
investments by CRAFT.

1.5.3
49.
While substantial engagement has taken place at international level, in-country
stakeholder assessment is much less clear, although the AE has clarified and expanded on their
plans to strengthen it in response to the TAP’s questions. This lack of clarity is not surprising,
given the relatively open nature of the programme and the fact that much of the stakeholder
consultation would have to be undertaken by investee companies, rather than the AE or EE, in
order for it to be meaningful. The provision of a real-life example from a portfolio company will
be valuable insight in this regard.
50.
Nevertheless, in the view of the TAP, it will be important for Pegasus to ensure that full
in-country stakeholder engagement takes place as part of CRAFT’s due diligence process (i.e.
pr ior to any investments being made):
(a)
By CRAFT in investee companies, and
(b)
By investee companies in countries.
51.
Following this, the AE’s observing member in the Investment Committee and CRAFT’s
member in an investee company’s board should, in the TAP’s view, not support or vote (as
applicable) in favour of any investment unless the AE and the EE, respectively, are comfortable
that stakeholders have been fully consulted and that meaningful action in response to the
co nsultation has taken place.
52.
The TAP would suggest that, similar to the ex-ante assessment of the impact potential of
investee companies, a structure and detailed reporting tool for in-country stakeholder
engagement could strengthen this aspect further, allowing others to assess, for instance, the
nature and number of stakeholder contacts broken down by gender, the nature of feedback
receive d anCdo uthnet rayc toiownns etarskhenip t ora atdindgre ss such feedback.
1.5.4
53.
Considering the above, the TAP rates country ownership as MEDIUM/HIGH.

55.

56.
Furthermore, it is not even certain that the GCF funds will be used fully in support of
GCF objectives. The TAP recalls that the Governing Instrument defines the activities of GCF in
Article I. 1. as related to climate while Article V. B. 35 specifies that all developing country
Parties to the Convention are eligible to receive GCF funds. Neither of these two elements is, in
the TAP’s view, appropriately reflected in the eligibility criteria of the GCF investment in CRAFT,
as other development impacts are eligible and the allocation of only a “substantial” part of the
GCF investment to NOL-issuing countries is allowed. Given the way the criteria have been
worded, no certainty can be derived that the GCF investment would be directed towards the
purposes outlined in the Governing Instrument. As such, no co-financing ratio can be calculated,
althoug h thGeo avmerbnitaionnc eo f the AE and the EE are noted.
1.6.2
57.
The TAP notes the AE response that the expectation is for Pegasus to be allowed by
other investors to join CRAFT’s LPAC and to be given an observer seat on CRAFT’s Investment
Committee. Given that GCF is providing 25 per cent of the CRAFT Fund’s capital, the TAP is of
the view that a non-negotiable condition of GCF’s investment should be that the AE obtains and
maintains—throughout the life of the GCF investment in CRAFT—a voting seat on the LPAC and
an observer seat on the Investment Committee, as this will enable the AE to observe during the
ass essent of investments directly with a qualified member of its staff.
58.
In relation to governance, the TAP would therefore like to note that, even if all
substantial matters on eligibility and investee governance were to be addressed satisfactorily, of
critical importance is the appointment as observer of a qualified senior staff member or
nominee of the AE to the CRAFT Investment Committee or whichever structure is carrying out
the functions typically undertaken by an Investment Committee. The qualification of such an
appointee would probably need to be in climate science, adaptation or a closely related field,
and the member should be of sufficient experience and seniority to adequately provide
safegu ardsW foart tehrefa GllC sFt irnuvcetsutrmee nt in the Investment Committee.
1.6.3
59.
The utilization of a concessional tranche to entice private sector investment and the
creation of a waterfall structure for income distribution, which provides the lowest income to
the highest risk share in fund equity, is a well-established mechanism and certainly has use in
addressing climate challenges. A successful example of such a structure is the Green for Growth
Fu nd established by the European Investment Bank and KfW Development Bank in 2009.
60.
The TAP notes however that the modelling of returns may be optimistic, given the
nature of the targeted companies, at least some of which may be at a stage in their growth

=== PAGE 87 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.13
Page 84
where there is a continuing high risk of failure, and thus complete loss of the investment. This is
no t an inappropriate risk for GCF to bear.
61.
Nevertheless, the willingness to bear such risk and accept the suggested return
structure should in turn be met by absolute clarity on the eligibility criteria side, ensuring that
the objectives of the provider of these funds are met in full. This is not the case as proposed in
the funding proposal document and draft term sheet, since, as discussed in paragraph 16. above,
it is entirely possible, based on the investment criteria provided to the TAP, that little or even
none of the GCF funds invested in a particular company through CRAFT would be allocated to
support the achievement of GCF objectives. The TAP has therefore suggested a range of
conditi onsE afnfidc iceonvceyn aanntds feofrfe acdtoivpetinoens bs yr athtien Bgo ard to address this.
1.6.4
62.
In view of the above considerations, the effectiveness and efficiency of the project is
rated as LOW/MEDIUM.

II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel
63.
The development of the CRAFT fund as a vehicle for investment dedicated to support
adaptation technologies through investing in early stage companies is highly welcome and, with
the right approach to eligibility, country ownership, localization and governance, this could be
aligned with the objectives of the GCF and the needs of the countries in which the CRAFT Fund
wi ll invest GCF resources.
64.
The initial assessment of the TAP critically considered the questions of eligibility,
country ownership, governance and capacity of the AE and the EE, as well as climate rationale
and measurement, reporting and verification (MRV). Based on this, a range of questions was
rai sed to the AE, to which responses were received that clarified some of the matters.
65.
The AE managed to address critical matters to the satisfaction of the TAP, but we do
note that there is no legal certainty regarding these commitments, and we are therefore only in
a position to recommend this funding proposal for approval by the Board with a range of
co nditions and covenants attached.
66.
The TAP remains of the view that a dedicated feasibility study, even in short form, would
have most likely raised many of the questions the TAP is raising in this assessment, and would
have allowed the AE to address them prior to submission. It is hard to see how, given the critical
importance of the GCF’s investment in CRAFT to enable it to substantially scale up operations,
the development of such a study was not considered a high priority.
67.

submitted for approval by the CRAFT Investment Committee demonstrates how the investee
company and the use of GCF proceeds in the investee company will achieve the country

Impact potential
The AE would like to thank the ITAP for its review of the impact potential of the CRAFT Programme and
its acknowledgment of the Adaptation Solutions Taxonomy as a first filter in order to confirm that a
particular technology constitutes a “resilience technology solution”.
The AE welcomes the suggestions to further strengthen the impact potential, including changing the word
“substantial” in the eligibility criteria to “100%” of GCF proceeds to be spent or invested in Host Countries
and the Climate Adaptation Impact Assessment Tool, to further deepen and formalize the CRAFT Fund’s
identification of specific climate vulnerabilities to be addressed in Host Countries and indicators to track
Panadr ardepigomrt sohni fctl ipmoatteen atdiaalp tation impacts resulting from these investments.
The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the paradigm shift potential of the CRAFT Programme, and it
welcomes the suggestions to further strengthen the paradigm shift potential through refinements to the
eligibility criteria, governance arrangements, and ex-ante assessment methods.
Sustainable development potential
The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the sustainable development potential of the CRAFT Programme,
and it welcomes the suggestions to further strengthen the paradigm shift potential through the
implementation in each proposed investment of the Climate Adaptation Impact Assessment Tool. The AE
agrees with ITAP that the Climate Adaptation Impact Assessment Tool should (a) identify the need for
specific climate adaptation solutions to address climate vulnerabilities in each Host Country and (b)
assess the potential for adoption of the investee company’s technologies, products or services in each
Host Country. The AE notes that baseline market studies are not feasible for a blind pool fund that intend
to invest in 8-12 investee companies with different technologies, in different sectors, in multiple Host
Countries, especially in the new field of climate adaptation technology investing. The AE agrees that the
ITAP-recommended Climate Adaptation Impact Assessment Tool helps to formalize assessment of the
Needs of the recipient
sustainable development and climate adaptation potential in Host Countries.
The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the needs of the recipient in the CRAFT Programme, and it notes
ITAP’s assessment that the needs of developing country recipients are “high” for climate adaptation
technologies and solutions. The AE welcomes the suggestions to further strengthen the fit with the needs
of the recipient, and in particular the domestic applicability and fit with these needs in Host Countries,
through the implementation in each proposed investment of the Climate Adaptation Impact Assessment

AOsvseersaslml reenmt Taorokls. from the independent Technical Advisory Panel
The AE team would like to thank the ITAP for its review and assessment of the CRAFT Programme and
notes with thanks its positive recommendation to the GCF Board to approve the CRAFT Programme.
The AE further confirms its agreement with the principles of the recommendations of the ITAP for
development of the CRAFT Programme. The AE recommends GCF Board approval conditioned on the
following points consistent with the ITAP’s recommendations:
1. The AE confirms that it will be granted a voting seat on the CRAFT Fund’s Limited Partnership
Advisory Committee, consistent with the ITAP’s recommendation.
2. The AE confirms that together with the Secretariat and the CRAFT Fund, the AE will ensure that
companies that receive investment from the GCF shall contractually commit to spend or invest
100% of GCF proceeds for eligible activities that will generate impacts in in Host Countries,
consistent with ITAP’s recommendation.
3. The AE confirms that it will contractually secure from the CRAFT Fund the right to observe and
consult on each investment that proposes to use GCF proceeds, specifically with respect to
environmental and social standards and the assessment results of the Climate Adaptation Impact
Assessment Tool, consistent with the ITAP’s recommendation for observer rights.
The AE notes that to avoid potential legal liability for the GCF and AE and concerns of existing and
potential investors, the observation and consultation rights will explicitly exclude involvement in the
investment decision-making process, consistent with industry practice. The AE believes that the
observation and consultation right addresses ITAP’s concern about ensuring that CRAFT Fund investee
companies will use GCF proceeds to generate impacts in Host Countries while safeguarding against
environmental and social risk, without creating legal liability exposure for the GCF or the AE, or
dissuading existing or potential investors from co-financing the GCF.
4. The AE confirms that it will contractually ensure that GCF Proceeds invested through the CRAFT
Fund will only be invested in companies that provide the CRAFT Fund with active governance
rights, including a Board seat or a Board observer seat and such contractual rights, reporting
rights, remedies, and/or other industry standard active governance rights, consistent with the
ITAP’s recommendations around CRAFT Fund governance.
The AE appreciates the ITAP’s recommendation that GCF proceeds be focused on companies where the
CRAFT Fund secures a voting Board seat and commits to limiting the investment of GCF proceeds to those
companies and other companies that provide appropriate active governance rights. The AE notes that a
Board seat alone would not guarantee that a CRAFT Fund investee company would commit to spend or
invest 100% of GCF proceeds in eligible activities that generate impacts in Host Countries. Instead, the AE
commits to ensure that the CRAFT Fund secures the combination of active governance rights (including a
Board seat or Board observer seat) appropriate to each investment to generate the goals of the GCF.
5. The AE confirms that it will contractually ensure that it has right to obtain reporting from the
CRAFT Fund to implement and oversee the CRAFT Programme, including obtaining updated
reporting on the ongoing use of GCF proceeds and their impacts.

Gender Assessment
1. Overview
2. Project Sector Category Assessments
a. Agriculture & Food Security
b. Water Availability & Efficiency
3. Approach to Gender-Responsive Impact Measurement
4. Gender Responsive Project Design
5. Fund Application
1. Overview
The Climate Resilience and Adaptation Finance & Technology Transfer Facility (CRAFT) seeks to
invest in technologies that can help communities assess and manage "key climate
vulnerabilities", which are defined as significant risk of loss, damage, sets of physical assets, or
economic ecological systems resulting from physical phenomena, all of which have a
disproportionate impact on women and girls. CRAFT accomplishes this by investing in climate
resilience "tools" that can help assess and manage climate risks, particularly those resulting in
direct impacts for marginalized populations. CRAFT contributes to reducing gender gaps in
access to climate mitigation products and services as well as those who benefit from enhanced
protections against climate change. Across areas as diverse as agriculture, water, food security,
and disaster risk management, CRAFT’s focus on climate resiliency strives to disproportionately
benefit those most at risk, including women, by supporting products and innovations that are
inclusive of protecting women’s livelihoods.
To track its impacts, CRAFT has established an Impact Measurement System (IMS), which
evaluates a set of core indicators for each investment and disaggregates impacts based on
gender, as applicable. Impact measurement is integrated into all stages of the investment
process: from screening, assessment, investment decision, the investment agreement,
monitoring and reporting and the eventual exit. The measurement process includes selecting
indicators, collecting and analyzing data on these indicators from investee companies, and
using the results in decision-making and reporting. CRAFT measures its impact against relevant
SDGs, including: climate mitigation, gender, life on land, and other social and economic co-
benefits through the identification of key performance indicators. In addition, for each
investment, the fund estimates the following types of direct and indirect beneficiaries
disaggregated by gender, to the extent possible:
• Employee beneficiaries (direct/indirect; full-time/part-time) – individuals employed by
the company directly and jobs created indirectly through the company’s products or
services.
• Client / user beneficiaries (direct/indirect) – individuals benefiting from the climate
resiliency products or services directly sold to them by the company or downstream

=== PAGE 93 ===
Page 90
individuals served by a customer of the company (enabled to do so by the company’s
product or service offering).
• Organizations trained –- organizations benefiting from capacity-building delivered by
the company’s products or services. Lightsmith will strive to publish sex-disaggregated
data on affiliated employees.
Impact measurement monitoring and reporting is integrated into the Fund’s Environmental and
Social Management System (ESMS) and consolidated and reported to CRAFT’s investors in an
Annual ESG & Impact Report. Based on the sex-disaggregated data provided by the portfolio
companies, and pending securing the required funding to set up a parallel technical assistance
facility, the project will enlist local consultants to conduct surveys, interviews and on-the
ground fact finding missions encompassing company beneficiaries, which will inform an annual
progress report on the gendered impacts of CRAFT’s engagements across the sub-sectors and
countries in which it has invested.
In order to streamline and guide its approach to closing gender gaps for the purposes of this
project, CRAFT’s Gender Assessment in conjunction with its Gender Action Plan will guide the
program’s fund and portfolio level approach to gender equality and women’s empowerment,
and address the links between gender equality, climate mitigation and adaptation, and
environmental sustainability. This assessment will translate its objectives at both the fund and
project level through indicators and targets to support CRAFT’s commitments to, and
engagement with, women, men, girls and boys, including those from marginalized
communities. The Assessment and Action Plan are underpinned by CRAFTs Environmental
Social Management System (ESMS) and Impact Measurement System (IMS) as well as its
Gender Mainstreaming Policy.
The Assessment builds upon quantitative data and qualitative evidence derived from
Lightsmith’s impact policies and procedures as well as desk review of existing literature focused
particularly on the sectors targeted by CRAFT for its investments. The Gender Assessment and
Action Plan are iterative guidance tools, which will be regularly updated, particularly as the
fund prepares for a new investment and monitors the performances of its portfolio companies.
Given that the countries and companies which CRAFT may invest in have not yet been
identified, the Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan will be updated and revised in
conjunction with both target portfolio companies as well as subsectors and countries of
investment. Additionally, CRAFT will be guided by its own Impact Measurement System, which
collects sex-disaggregated data, where possible, to inform its efforts to close existing gender
gaps.
2. Project Sector Category Assessments:
The overall purpose of the project is to catalyze the markets for climate resilience and
adaptation solutions in developing countries and promote greater use of these solutions by
customers. Developing the market for resilience and adaptation solutions, especially in

=== PAGE 94 ===
Page 91
developing countries, has a direct impact on women. Climate risks and impacts (such as
catastrophic weather events, water scarcity, climate impacts on food and agriculture, climate-
related healthcare impact) disproportionately affect women and girls. For example, a London
School of Economics (2007) study found that five times more women than men die from
natural disasters due to inequalities in exposure and sensitivity to risk, and especially so in
developing countries. Through its investment thesis CRAFT will increase the availability and
awareness of technologies, products, and solutions that can help to assess and mitigate these
risks and impacts to which women are especially exposed. Throughout the project, Lightsmith
will capture the gender impact through Lightsmith/CRAFT’s impact measurement system. The
data collected will help to build an understanding of the differentiated impact that adaptation
technologies, products, and solutions can have on women and men.
The initial core subsectors that CRAFT targets have been found to be disproportionately
significant on women. Core subsectors in which CRAFT is seeking to invest have distinct gender
gaps, which can be alleviated through broader access to climate risk mitigation technologies in
the form of products, services, and data analytics:
• Agricultural and Food Security (SDG 2) - Globally, according to reports by the
United Nations' Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), the majority of economically
active women in the least-developed countries work in agriculture1. Similarly, the
United Nations has reported that close to 66% of women in developing countries work
in the agricultural sector2.
• Water availability and efficiency (SDG 6) - A 2017 UNICEF report found that women and
girls are responsible for water collection in 80 percent of households.3 Water collection
not only represents a major source of unpaid domestic work, but also has implications
for women’s health in terms increased frequency of injuries to the neck and spine from
heavy workload. Girls’ development is also particularly impacted as they carry water
over long distances, which results in reduced time available for education and the
acquisition of skills. Thus, by carrying the burden of water gathering, adolescent girls are
also far less likely than boys to have time for income-generating opportunities4.
Reductions in time spent for water collection responsibilities has been found to boost
women's economic activity, according to a recent study.5
1 FAO Stat, Food and Agriculture Data, http://www.fao.org/faostat/en/#home
2 “The Need for Gender Sensitive Approaches to Climate Change”, UN,
https://www.un.org/womenwatch/feature/climate_change/downloads/Women_and_Climate_Change_Factsheet.pdf
3 UN Women. Turning Promises into Action: Gender Equality in the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development (New York, 2018).

=== PAGE 95 ===
Page 92
• Resilience to extreme weather events (SDG 11) – Extreme weather events such as
droughts and floods have a greater impact on the poor and most vulnerable – 70% of
the world’s poor are women6. Disasters hit the poorest and most marginalized
demographics the hardest. Women and girls are particularly exposed to climate-related
disaster risk and they are likely to suffer higher rates of mortality, morbidity and
economic damage to their livelihoods7. Some evidence suggests that domestic and
sexual violence against women increases due to disaster-induced stress and chaos
ensuing from post-event responses8. An Oxfam report suggests that across Sri Lanka,
Indonesia, and India men were three times more likely to survive the 2004
tsunami9. The study sheds light on the reality that the additional care responsibilities
shouldered by women, both in terms of looking after children and the elderly, have an
impact on women’s ability and time available to evacuate disaster-stricken areas and
reduce their likelihood of survival in comparison to men. Research suggests that women
are more risk-aware and at the forefront of developing innovative solutions to climate
adaptation10, suggesting they may disproportionately benefit from the scaling of climate
resilience focused technologies and services that help inform their behaviors.
Sector: Agriculture and Food Security:
Data suggests that in low-income and lower middle-income countries, agriculture is still the
most important source of employment for women. Almost 70 percent of employed women in
South Asia and more than 60 percent of employed women in Sub-Saharan Africa work in
agriculture. The high representation of rural women in agriculture highlights the importance of
developing policies and programs that address the needs, interests and constraints of women
in this sector11.
Rural women play a significant role in supporting their families and communities in achieving
food security, generating income, and improving rural livelihoods. Yet although rural women
and girls make up a significant part of the agricultural labor force, they are not only much less
likely to own the land on which they work to derive their livelihoods, but they also have access
to fewer value-add assets such as human capital and the analytical tools to inform their work
and to maximize the benefits of their labor12. In addition, as in some developing countries,
women’s contributions to the agricultural sector exceeds 50%, they are much more likely to be
exposed to the adverse effects of climate change.
6 “Policy Coherence for Sustainable Development 2019 Empowering people and Ensuring Inclusiveness and Equality”, OECD,
2019
7 “Gender and Disaster Risk Reduction, UNDP, 2013

10 “Women and Climate Change: Impact and Agency in Human Rights, Security, and Economic Development”,
https://giwps.georgetown.edu/wp-content/uploads/2017/09/Women-and-Climate-Change.pdf

12 World Bank, https://www.worldbank.org/en/topic/agriculture/brief/women-farmers-getting-to-equal

Page 93
Increasing rates of soil loss and land degradation, impacts of drought, flooding, heat waves and
wildfires will only continue to compound the risks faced by women in the agriculture sector.
Concurrently, demographic trends are set to increase the demand for food and agricultural
products, placing more pressure on a system, which provides little risk mitigation to those
underserved13. The combination of a threatened supply and enhanced demand is projected to
increase the number of pressure points on land usuage and have a detrimental impact on the
livlihoods of women within the agricultural sector. Additionally, climate risks add to the already
socio-economic and cultural stresses women already face; from the unequal distribution of
child and elderly care responsibilities, to extensive unpaid responsibilities in the household, and
less stable income-generating opportunities. For example, jobs of rural women employed in the
agricultural sector are often shorter-term and less protected by social safety nets than those of
rural men14. The lack of flexible hours to accommodate family work combined with wage and
job discrimination and limited representation of women in workers' organizations are key
factors driving this trend. These stresses have an acute effect on women’s exposure to financial
risks, which are only further compounded by the impacts of climate change on the productivity
of agricultural lands15.
Given that women are overrepresented in terms of those working in agriculture, they are
disadvantaged in comparison to men in terms of their access to vital inputs such as seeds,
fertilizer, finance and services such as climate risk insurance, training and other services which
may provide protection to their livelihoods16. By providing women with more equal access to
inputs – such as products, services, tailored insurance plans that meet the particular needs of
women – has the potential shrink this persistent gender gap, but also to increase yields on
women-run farms by 20 – 30%, in addition to potentially raising total agricultural output in
developing countries by close to 4%.17 Climate change exacerbates the rate and magnitude of
land degradation with approximately 25% of the total global land area already affected. Each
year, roughly 75 billion tons of fertile soil and 12 million hectares of econologically or
agriculturally productive land are degraded through desertification and drought alone. Globally,
2 billion people are affected by land degradation. Given that women are overrepresented in the
agricultural sector, gender gaps are continually exacerbated by land degradation processes and
have increasingly impacted rural communities, smallholder farmers, and the very poor, a
majority of which are women.18 Land and forest degradation processes threaten the
livelihoods, well-being, food, water, and energy security, increase the vulnerability of millions of
13 “The Future of Food and Agriculture”, The Trends and Challenges, FAO, http://www.fao.org/3/i6583e/i6583e.pdf 2017
14 “Rural Women and the Millennium Development Goals”, UN, https://www.un.org/womenwatch/feature/ruralwomen/facts-
figures.html
15 “The Business Case for Women’s Employment in Agribusiness”, IFC, World Bank Group,

women and children, and in many cases cause mass migration and social unrest, further
compounding the risks women face to their livlihoods, health and well-being19.
Unfortunately, agricultural production practices in many countries, particularly in emerging
markets have not adpated to the accelerating trends and impacts of land degradation on
communities and those marginalized. The sector has been relatively unmalleable to adopting
new practices, not only in terms of land ownership, but also in providing more equal access to
risk mitigation and climate adaptation tools, which would help protect the livelihoods of more
underserved workers, particularly women. CRAFT is committed to supporting impactful
innovations and technologies, which will help alleviate the impacts of land degradation or other
processes accelerated by climate change, which disrupt the livelihoods of those dependent on
agriculture.
An additioal example within the agricultural secotor relates to food security. A 2019 IPCC report
states that climate change will precipitate severe food shortages and reduce food’s nutritional
quality. In addition to increasing the rate of soil loss and land degradation, impacts of drought,
flooding heat waves and wildfires threaten the sector more broadly. Growing population and
affluence will increase demand for both the quantity and quality of food and agricultural
commodities. The combination of a threatened supply and growing demand will put pressure
on productive land, even while competition for that land increases from biofuels, urban
expansion and other non-productive uses.
CRAFT seeks to also invest in enabled physical products, such as drought-tolerant crops and
drip irrigation systems. These types of solutions improve sustainable management of land,
while building resilience to climate-related vulnerabilities that are reducing agricultural
productivity and increasing food import dependence. As these climate resilience solutions are
scaled up, they can be integrated as components of systemic interventions designed to
generate additional global environmental benefits in the context of physical climate change
risks. In addition to its investments in high-impact, innovative companies which provide the
products, services, or access to data to equip farmers and those in the agricultural sector with
the tools to mitigate these climate risks, CRAFT is envisioning establishing a technical assistance
facility (contingent upon securing the necessary funding), which would take concrete steps in
providing advisory services to companies and governments to revamp and strengthen
extension systems to be more responsive to and inclusive of women, addressing structural
barriers to ensure more equitable access to productive resources, while helping companies
better respond to the needs of rural women. This will include supporting more women to
access the tools to protect themselves and their families from the ripple effects of land
19 “A Special Report on Climate Change and Land Degradation”, The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change,
https://www.ipcc.ch/srccl/chapter/chapter-4/

=== PAGE 98 ===
Page 95
degradation on their livelihoods as well as to ensure their mobility and access to risk mitigation
products that enable them to secure their futures20.
Sector: Water Availability and Efficiency:
Women are not just underrepresented in specialized jobs, they are overrepresented in unpaid
work, including water collection. Across Sub-Saharan Africa women are far more likely to gather
water then men, especially in households that lack piped water within the home21. For
example, the World Bank estimates that in Niger, each woman spends on average two weeks a
year traveling to and from a water source. In rural areas, the time spent gathering water is
especially high.22 In rural areas of Guinea, for example, women spend more than twice as much
time collecting water per week than men, while in Malawi they spend over eight times more
than men on the same tasks. Collectively, women from Sub-Saharan Africa spend about 40
billion hours a year collecting water for their families and communities, which is equivalent to a
year’s worth of labor by the entire workforce in France.23 Innovations which leverage
technology to enhance climate resilience while alleviating stresses on those particularly
burdened by water scarcity have the potential to accelerate the closing of the gender gaps in
the water sector. Water harvesting, in particular, can enhance water security by improving
household health, reductions in time spent and distances traveled to fetch water, while also
adapting to climate change.24
Companies that leverage technology to provide access to low-cost water harvesting tools have
the potential to boost the livelihoods of women and children by reducing the time spent on
gathering water, and thus contribute to closing significant gender gaps. CRAFT investment
interests in technology-enabled physical products and services encompass drip irrigation
systems and water harvesting panels. These types of solutions have many positive effects in
adapting to climate change while also addressing SDGs focused on clean water and sanitation,
and gender equality. As these climate resilience solutions are scaled up, they can be integrated
as components of systemic interventions designed to generate additional impact in the context
of physical climate change risk. Ultimately, CRAFT’s investments in climate resilience solutions
can help deliver impact, while reducing the vulnerability of people, physical assets and natural
systems to the adverse effects of climate change. Further, by applying the IMS, Lightsmith will
track, to the extent possible, the gendered impact of investments to understand the
measurable impacts firms and their products have on women and girls and the potential to
shrink sector specific gender gaps. Given the outsized impact that climate risks have on women,

Tracking climate change adaptation and resilience impact is complicated by the broad array of
sectors, subsectors, and geographies to which adaptation applies, as well as a lack of commonly
agreed impact indicators given the nascence of adaptation as an investment opportunity. The
impact potential of each investment in shrinking existing gender gaps is likely to differ
depending on a number of factors, such as the country and subsector of investment, the acute
vulnerabilities faced by women and girls in that subsector and the geographic location.
The United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (“SDGs”), which were formed in 2015, have
been accepted as an internationally recognized set of goals, targets, and indicators to guide the
global development agenda through 2030. Lightsmith utilizes the SDGs to provide additional
high-level references to anchor climate action in the broader policy sphere as well as a common
framework for communicating other impacts. The project will engage with the SDGs at the
following points in its investment process:
• Investment Strategy- The SDGs provide a framework to drive change through the
investment strategy, which is fundamentally rooted in promoting SDG 13, Climate
Action with additional impacts to other areas of the SDGs, including SDG 5, and Gender
Equality.
• Mapping Investments to SDGs- At the time of investment, the activities of each
Portfolio Company will be assessed based on their expected contribution to specific
Sustainable Development Goals, including SDG 5, Gender Equality and Targets and

Lightsmith will strive to include a gender-sensitive framework, articulate gender-responsive
elements of its investments. The project’s monitoring framework includes gender-responsive
targets and indicators to monitor gender equality results against the established baseline.
4. Gender Responsive Project Design:
It is impossible to integrate gender equality into projects or activities without clearly
understanding gender issues within the targeted population. The gender-responsive design of
the project will be guided by its Gender Action Plan and will be based on understanding the
social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequality.
The Gender Assessment and Action Plan will be adjusted and updated as the project
progresses. Building on the safeguards and consultations, CRAFT will require prospective
projects to establish and commit to the following mandatory, minimum gender-responsive
requirements:
• Collect, analyze and apply sex-disaggregated data and using gender indicators to inform
gender- responsive monitoring reporting, where possible.
• Analyze risks that the project may experience or pose, putting measures in place to
ensure that activities do not exacerbate existing gender-related inequalities, including
gender-based violence, and seize opportunities to address gender gaps and support the
empowerment of women.
• Through a potential technical assistance facility (funding would need to be
secured), allocate sufficient resources for specific activities, advisory support and/or
other actions to improve gender equality considerations, including the meaningful
engagement of diverse stakeholders and beneficiaries.
5. Fund Application:
The Assessment and Gender Action Plan provide guidance to CRAFT’s approach in its
investment portfolio and the Fund’s operations. Specifically, the Assessment has been used to
inform the following areas within the Fund:
• Governance: responsibilities that inform CRAFT’s Gender Mainstreaming Plan
• Capacity to apply the Gender Action Plan

=== PAGE 102 ===
Page 99
• Additional resources required to support a possible technical assistance facility, which
would enhance CRAFT’s gender analyses, particularly at the portfolio level.
• Operational procedures – at both the Fund level and in monitoring portfolio companies.
Encompassing the inclusion of a gender assessment as part of the ESMS for each
project, and gender-responsive stakeholder consultation requirements and safeguards.
• Capturing knowledge and enhancing thought leadership.

Annex 8: Gender Action Plan
Impact Statement: The CRAFT Fund seeks to invest in technologies that can help communities, particularly those
marginalized – including women and girls, in developing countries suffer less damage, loss, harm, and disruption (and
recover more effectively when harm and disruption occur). CRAFT mobilizes capital and invests in technologies to enable
vulnerable populations, including women, to identify, mitigate, and manage risks from climate change. Concurrently, it seeks to
create opportunities to enhance livelihoods, particularly for women. In ensuring equitable access to the benefits of the project,
including employment and income generating activities, the Fund will identify potential impact, technical assistance, or other
available donor funds where possible to support disenfranchised groups, including women, to access climate resiliency tools and
knowledge products to facilitate climate-smart innovation. CRAFT will enable local businesses, including women-owned and/or led
enterprises to adapt their existing methods, expand local markets and demand, as well as attract further investment, especially for yet
to be identified adaptation products and services.
Outcome Statement: CRAFT will reduce climate vulnerabilities for women and contribute to boosting income-generating
opportunities by scaling access to localized, asset-specific information on climate-related risks and impacts, and physical products
and services to manage those risks and impacts. CRAFT will achieve the following gender-responsive outcomes and report sex-
disaggregated fund level impacts to the extent possible.
Sustainable Cities and Communities: Empowerment and resilience of vulnerable populations, including women and girls, through
strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.
Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities, and regions. Opportunities benefiting
women and girls who are often disproportionately disadvantaged, will build resilience through enhanced access to resources and
economic engagement.
Increased resilience of health and well-being, food and water security for communities, including women and girls. More resilient
water systems from investments in distributed, data-based, and efficient water technologies will enhance access to reliable and safe
water supply, despite climate shocks and stresses, which will benefit vulnerable populations, including women and girls.

--- Table 1 on Page 105 ---
Annex 8: Gender Action Plan
Impact Statement: The CRAFT Fund seeks to invest in technologies that can help communities, particularly those
